Combinatorial Solving and Optimisation

                 Revolution last couple of decades in combinatorial solvers for
                        Boolean satisfiability (SAT) solving [BHvMW21]1
                        Constraint programming (CP) [RvBW06]
                        Mixed integer linear programming (MIP) [AW13, BR07]

                 Solve NP-complete problems (or worse) very successfully in practice!
                 Except solvers are sometimes wrong. . . (Even best commercial ones)
                 [BLB10, CKSW13, AGJ+ 18, GSD19, BMN22, GCS23]
                 Solvers can propose infeasible “solutions” (but such errors can be detected)
                 More challenging: How to achieve reliable claims of infeasibility?
                 Or that a solution is optimal? (Even off-by-one mistakes can snowball into large errors if
                 solver used as subroutine)
               1 See end of slides for all references with bibliographic details
Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                               The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    4/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                   End of First Half




What Can Be Done About Solver Bugs?

                Software testing
                Very useful, but bugs slip through even with careful domain-specific testing
                Progress using fuzzing and delta debugging [BB09, BLB10, KB22, NPB22, PB23]
                But testing inherently can only detect presence of bugs, not absence




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    5/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                   End of First Half




What Can Be Done About Solver Bugs?

                Software testing
                Very useful, but bugs slip through even with careful domain-specific testing
                Progress using fuzzing and delta debugging [BB09, BLB10, KB22, NPB22, PB23]
                But testing inherently can only detect presence of bugs, not absence
                Formal methods
                Prove that solver implementation adheres to formal specification
                Current formal verification techniques cannot scale to level of complexity in modern solvers
                (Despite valiant efforts in, e.g., [Fle20])




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    5/128
Introduction                   Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                   End of First Half




What Can Be Done About Solver Bugs?

                Software testing
                Very useful, but bugs slip through even with careful domain-specific testing
                Progress using fuzzing and delta debugging [BB09, BLB10, KB22, NPB22, PB23]
                But testing inherently can only detect presence of bugs, not absence
                Formal methods
                Prove that solver implementation adheres to formal specification
                Current formal verification techniques cannot scale to level of complexity in modern solvers
                (Despite valiant efforts in, e.g., [Fle20])
                Proof logging
                Make solver certifying [ABM+ 11, MMNS11] by adding code so that it outputs
                    1   not only answer but also
                    2   simple, machine-verifiable proof that answer is correct

Some Previous Proof Logging Work
         Boolean satisfiability (SAT) solving
            Well established since over decade with several proof formats such as
             DRAT [HHW13a, HHW13b, WHH14], GRIT [CMS17], LRAT [CHH+ 17], . . .
             But no efficient support for most advanced techniques such as
                        Gaussian elimination
                        symmetry breaking
                And only for decision problems, not optimization (MaxSAT)




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    8/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                   End of First Half




Some Previous Proof Logging Work
         Boolean satisfiability (SAT) solving
            Well established since over decade with several proof formats such as
             DRAT [HHW13a, HHW13b, WHH14], GRIT [CMS17], LRAT [CHH+ 17], . . .
             But no efficient support for most advanced techniques such as
                        Gaussian elimination
                        symmetry breaking
                And only for decision problems, not optimization (MaxSAT)
         Constraint programming
            Either have to trust that propagations done correctly [DFS12, OSC09, VS10]
            Or suffer from exponential slow-down to generate verifiable proofs [GCS23]




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    8/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                   End of First Half




Some Previous Proof Logging Work
         Boolean satisfiability (SAT) solving
            Well established since over decade with several proof formats such as
             DRAT [HHW13a, HHW13b, WHH14], GRIT [CMS17], LRAT [CHH+ 17], . . .
             But no efficient support for most advanced techniques such as
                        Gaussian elimination
                        symmetry breaking
                And only for decision problems, not optimization (MaxSAT)
         Constraint programming
            Either have to trust that propagations done correctly [DFS12, OSC09, VS10]
            Or suffer from exponential slow-down to generate verifiable proofs [GCS23]
         Mixed integer linear programming
            Work on proof format VIPR [CGS17, EG23]
            But only for exact solving and without support for advanced techniques

Take-Away Message from This Tutorial

         Proof logging for sophisticated combinatorial solvers is possible!

Take-Away Message from This Tutorial

         Proof logging for sophisticated combinatorial solvers is possible!
                With single, unified method!
                Producing proofs in simple format calculating with 0–1 integer linear inequalities
                (a.k.a. pseudo-Boolean constraints)
                Implemented in VeriPB (https://VeriPB.org)

Design Principles for Proof Logging

         Proof logging implementation
                Don’t change solver
                Just add proof logging print statements (plus some book-keeping) to solver code




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    12/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




Design Principles for Proof Logging

         Proof logging implementation
                Don’t change solver
                Just add proof logging print statements (plus some book-keeping) to solver code

         Performance goals
                Proof logging overhead small constant fraction of running time (⪅ 10%)
                Proof checking time within constant factor of solving time (current aim ⪅ ×10)




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    12/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




Design Principles for Proof Logging

         Proof logging implementation
                Don’t change solver
                Just add proof logging print statements (plus some book-keeping) to solver code

         Performance goals
                Proof logging overhead small constant fraction of running time (⪅ 10%)
                Proof checking time within constant factor of solving time (current aim ⪅ ×10)

         Proof system
                Keep language simple — no XOR constraints, CP propagators, symmetry groups, . . .
                But reason efficiently about such notions using power of proof system
                Combine proof logging with formally verified proof checker

Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    12/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




But Let Us Start from the Beginning. . .



         Review of some basic concepts:
                Satisfiability (SAT) problem
                Unit propagation
                DPLL and CDCL algorithms
                Resolution proof system




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    13/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT                  Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




The Satisfiability (SAT) Problem
                Variable 𝑥: takes value true (= 1) or false (= 0)
                Literal ℓ: variable 𝑥 or its negation 𝑥
                Clause 𝐶 = ℓ1 ∨ · · · ∨ ℓ𝑘 : disjunction of literals
                (Consider as sets, so no repetitions and order irrelevant)
                Conjunctive normal form (CNF) formula 𝐹 = 𝐶 1 ∧ · · · ∧ 𝐶𝑚 : conjunction of clauses

         The SAT Problem
         Given a CNF formula 𝐹 , is it satisfiable?

         For instance, what about:
                                                  (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢) ∧ (𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 ) ∧ (𝑟 ∨ 𝑤) ∧ (𝑢 ∨ 𝑥 ∨ 𝑦) ∧
                                            (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢)

Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                               The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    14/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




Proofs for SAT



         For satisfiable instances: just specify satisfying assignment
         For unsatisfiability: a sequence of clauses (CNF constraints)
                Each clause follows “obviously” from everything we know so far
                Final clause is empty, meaning contradiction (written ⊥)
                Means original formula must be inconsistent




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    15/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




What Is Obvious? Unit Propagation

         Unit Propagation
         Clause 𝐶 unit propagates ℓ under partial assignment 𝜌 if 𝜌 falsifies all literals in 𝐶 except ℓ

What Is Obvious? Unit Propagation

         Unit Propagation
         Clause 𝐶 unit propagates ℓ under partial assignment 𝜌 if 𝜌 falsifies all literals in 𝐶 except ℓ


         Example: Unit propagate for 𝜌 = {𝑝 ↦→ 0, 𝑞 ↦→ 0} on our formula

         (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢) ∧ (𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 ) ∧ (𝑟 ∨ 𝑤) ∧ (𝑢 ∨ 𝑥 ∨ 𝑦) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢)

What Is Obvious? Unit Propagation

         Unit Propagation
         Clause 𝐶 unit propagates ℓ under partial assignment 𝜌 if 𝜌 falsifies all literals in 𝐶 except ℓ


         Example: Unit propagate for 𝜌 = {𝑝 ↦→ 0, 𝑞 ↦→ 0} on our formula

         (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢) ∧ (𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 ) ∧ (𝑟 ∨ 𝑤) ∧ (𝑢 ∨ 𝑥 ∨ 𝑦) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢)

                𝑝 ∨ 𝑢 propagates 𝑢 ↦→ 0
                𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 propagates 𝑟 ↦→ 1
                Then 𝑟 ∨ 𝑤 propagates 𝑤 ↦→ 1

What Is Obvious? Unit Propagation

         Unit Propagation
         Clause 𝐶 unit propagates ℓ under partial assignment 𝜌 if 𝜌 falsifies all literals in 𝐶 except ℓ


         Example: Unit propagate for 𝜌 = {𝑝 ↦→ 0, 𝑞 ↦→ 0} on our formula

         (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢) ∧ (𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 ) ∧ (𝑟 ∨ 𝑤) ∧ (𝑢 ∨ 𝑥 ∨ 𝑦) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢)

                𝑝 ∨ 𝑢 propagates 𝑢 ↦→ 0
                𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 propagates 𝑟 ↦→ 1
                Then 𝑟 ∨ 𝑤 propagates 𝑤 ↦→ 1
                No further unit propagations

Reverse Unit Propagation (RUP)

         To make this a proof, need backtrack clauses to be easily verifiable




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    18/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




Reverse Unit Propagation (RUP)

         To make this a proof, need backtrack clauses to be easily verifiable

         Reverse unit propagation (RUP) clause [GN03, Van08]
        𝐶 is a reverse unit propagation (RUP) clause with respect to 𝐹 if
                assigning 𝐶 to false
                then unit propagating on 𝐹 until saturation
                leads to contradiction
         If so, 𝐹 clearly implies 𝐶, and this condition is easy to verify efficiently




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    18/128

               𝑢 = 0                                                        d

               𝑧 = 1
                𝑦∨𝑧                                   Continue until satisfying assignment or conflict
                 ⊥

Conflict Analysis
         Time to analyse this conflict and learn from it!
         (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢) ∧ (𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 ) ∧ (𝑟 ∨ 𝑤) ∧ (𝑢 ∨ 𝑥 ∨ 𝑦) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢)
                 d
                𝑝 =0                  decision
                𝑝∨𝑢                   level 1
               𝑢 = 0
                 d
                𝑞 =0
                𝑞∨𝑟                   decision
               𝑟 = 1                  level 2
                𝑟 ∨𝑤
               𝑤 = 1
                 d
                𝑥 =0
               𝑢∨𝑥∨𝑦
           𝑦 = 1                      decision
               𝑥∨𝑦∨𝑧                  level 3
               𝑧 = 1
                𝑦∨𝑧
                 ⊥

         (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢) ∧ (𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 ) ∧ (𝑟 ∨ 𝑤) ∧ (𝑢 ∨ 𝑥 ∨ 𝑦) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢)
                 d
                𝑝 =0
                𝑝∨𝑢
               𝑢 = 0
                 d
                𝑞 =0
                𝑞∨𝑟
               𝑟 = 1
                𝑟 ∨𝑤
               𝑤 = 1
                 d
                𝑥 =0
               𝑢∨𝑥∨𝑦
           𝑦 = 1                     𝑢 ∨𝑥
               𝑥∨𝑦∨𝑧
               𝑧 = 1                 𝑥 ∨𝑦
                𝑦∨𝑧
                 ⊥

                 ⊥

CDCL Reasoning and the Resolution Proof System

         To describe CDCL reasoning, need formal proof system for unsatisfiable formulas




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    22/128

CDCL Reasoning and the Resolution Proof System

         To describe CDCL reasoning, need formal proof system for unsatisfiable formulas

         Resolution proof system [Bla37, Rob65]
                Start with clauses of formula (axioms)
                Derive new clauses by resolution rule

                                                                  𝐶 ∨𝑥    𝐷 ∨𝑥
                                                                      𝐶 ∨𝐷
                Done when contradiction ⊥ in form of empty clause derived




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    22/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




CDCL Reasoning and the Resolution Proof System

         To describe CDCL reasoning, need formal proof system for unsatisfiable formulas

         Resolution proof system [Bla37, Rob65]

                                                                  𝐶 ∨𝑥    𝐷 ∨𝑥
                                                                      𝐶 ∨𝐷
                Done when contradiction ⊥ in form of empty clause derived

         When run on unsatisfiable formula, CDCL generates resolution proof∗

CDCL Reasoning and the Resolution Proof System

         To describe CDCL reasoning, need formal proof system for unsatisfiable formulas

         Resolution proof system [Bla37, Rob65]
                Start with clauses of formula (axioms)
                Derive new clauses by resolution rule

                                                                     𝐶 ∨𝑥    𝐷 ∨𝑥
                                                                         𝐶 ∨𝐷
                Done when contradiction ⊥ in form of empty clause derived

         When run on unsatisfiable formula, CDCL generates resolution proof∗

         (*) Ignores pre- and inprocessing, but we will get there. . .

Resolution Proofs from CDCL Executions
         Obtain resolution proof from our example CDCL execution by stringing together conflict analyses:

                                                                                                                                       ⊥
                                                                                                                                       𝑥

                                                                                                       𝑝 ∨𝑢                            𝑢

                                                         𝑥 ∨𝑧                                   𝑥      𝑝 ∨𝑢

           𝑢 ∨𝑥 ∨𝑦                   𝑢 ∨𝑥
           𝑥 ∨𝑦 ∨𝑧                   𝑥 ∨𝑦

               𝑦 ∨𝑧

RUP Proofs and CDCL
         But it turns out we can be lazier. . .

         Fact
         All learned clauses generated by CDCL solver are RUP clauses


         So shorter proof of unsatisfiability for

         (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢) ∧ (𝑞 ∨ 𝑟 ) ∧ (𝑟 ∨ 𝑤) ∧ (𝑢 ∨ 𝑥 ∨ 𝑦) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑦 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑥 ∨ 𝑧) ∧ (𝑝 ∨ 𝑢)

         is sequence of reverse unit propagation (RUP) clauses
               1 𝑢 ∨𝑥
               2 𝑥

               3   ⊥

More Ingredients in Proof Logging for SAT



         Fact
         RUP proofs can be viewed as shorthand for resolution proofs


         See proof complexity and SAT solving survey [BN21] for more on this

         But RUP and resolution are not enough for preprocessing, inprocessing, and some other kinds of
         reasoning




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    25/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT               Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging          Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




Extension Variables, Part 1
         Suppose we want a reified variable 𝑟 encoding

                                                                  𝑟 ⇔ (𝑥 ∧ 𝑦)


         Extended resolution [Tse68]
         Resolution rule plus extension rule introducing clauses

                                                      𝑟 ∨𝑥 ∨𝑦                𝑟 ∨𝑥                𝑟 ∨𝑦

         for fresh variable 𝑟 (this is fine since 𝑟 doesn’t appear anywhere previously)




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                            The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                                ACP Summer School ’26    26/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT               Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging          Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




Extension Variables, Part 1
         Suppose we want a reified variable 𝑟 encoding

                                                                  𝑟 ⇔ (𝑥 ∧ 𝑦)


         Extended resolution [Tse68]
         Resolution rule plus extension rule introducing clauses

                                                      𝑟 ∨𝑥 ∨𝑦                𝑟 ∨𝑥                𝑟 ∨𝑦

         for fresh variable 𝑟 (this is fine since 𝑟 doesn’t appear anywhere previously)

         Fact
         Extended resolution (RUP + definition of new variables) is essentially equivalent to the DRAT proof
         logging system most commonly used for SAT solving [KRH18]

Why Aren’t We Done?
         Practical limitations of current SAT proof logging technology:
              Difficulties dealing with stronger reasoning efficiently (even for SAT solving)
              Clausal proofs can’t easily reflect what algorithms for other problems do

Why Aren’t We Done?
         Practical limitations of current SAT proof logging technology:
              Difficulties dealing with stronger reasoning efficiently (even for SAT solving)
              Clausal proofs can’t easily reflect what algorithms for other problems do

         Surprising claim: a slight change to 0-1 integer linear inequalities does the job!
             Enables proof logging for advanced SAT techniques so far beyond reach for efficient DRAT
             proof logging:
                        Cardinality reasoning
                        Gaussian elimination
                        Symmetry breaking

Why Aren’t We Done?
         Practical limitations of current SAT proof logging technology:
              Difficulties dealing with stronger reasoning efficiently (even for SAT solving)
              Clausal proofs can’t easily reflect what algorithms for other problems do

         Surprising claim: a slight change to 0-1 integer linear inequalities does the job!

             proof logging:
                        Cardinality reasoning
                        Gaussian elimination
                        Symmetry breaking
                Supports use of SAT solvers for optimisation problems (MaxSAT)
                Can justify graph reasoning without knowing what a graph is
                Can justify constraint programming inference without knowing what an integer variable is

Pseudo-Boolean Constraints


         0–1 integer linear inequalities or (linear) pseudo-Boolean constraints:
                                                       ∑︁
                                                          𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ 𝐴
                                                                     𝑖
                𝑎𝑖 , 𝐴 ∈ Z
                literals ℓ𝑖 : 𝑥𝑖 or 𝑥 𝑖 (where 𝑥𝑖 + 𝑥 𝑖 = 1)

Pseudo-Boolean Constraints


         0–1 integer linear inequalities or (linear) pseudo-Boolean constraints:
                                                       ∑︁
                                                          𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ 𝐴
                                                                     𝑖
                𝑎𝑖 , 𝐴 ∈ Z
                literals ℓ𝑖 : 𝑥𝑖 or 𝑥 𝑖 (where 𝑥𝑖 + 𝑥 𝑖 = 1)

         Sometimes convenient to use normalised form [Bar95] with all 𝑎𝑖 , 𝐴 positive
         (without loss of generality)

Some Types of Pseudo-Boolean Constraints


               1   Clauses
                                                      𝑥1 ∨ 𝑥 2 ∨ 𝑥3            ⇔          𝑥1 + 𝑥 2 + 𝑥3 ≥ 1


               2   Cardinality constraints
                                                                  𝑥1 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥3 + 𝑥4 ≥ 2


               3   General pseudo-Boolean constraints

                                                         𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 + 5𝑥 5 ≥ 7

Resolution and Cutting Planes
         To simulate resolution step such as
                                                              𝑦 ∨𝑧               𝑥 ∨𝑦 ∨𝑧
                                                                           𝑥 ∨𝑦
         we can perform the cutting planes steps
                                                            𝑦 +𝑧 ≥ 1                 𝑥 +𝑦 +𝑧 ≥ 1
                                                      Add
                                                                           𝑥 + 2𝑦 ≥ 1
                                                        Divide by 2
                                                                            𝑥 +𝑦 ≥ 1

Resolution and Cutting Planes
         To simulate resolution step such as
                                                              𝑦 ∨𝑧               𝑥 ∨𝑦 ∨𝑧
                                                                           𝑥 ∨𝑦
         we can perform the cutting planes steps
                                                            𝑦 +𝑧 ≥ 1                 𝑥 +𝑦 +𝑧 ≥ 1
                                                      Add
                                                                           𝑥 + 2𝑦 ≥ 1
                                                        Divide by 2
                                                                            𝑥 +𝑦 ≥ 1

         Given that the premises are clauses 7 and 5 in our example CNF formula, using references
                                                        Constraint 7  𝑦 + 𝑧 ≥ 1
                                                        Constraint 5  𝑥 + 𝑦 + 𝑧 ≥ 1
         we can write this in the proof log as
                                                              pol 7 5 + 2 d ;

RUP Revisited

         Can define (reverse) unit propagation in a pseudo-Boolean setting [EGMN20]
         Constraint 𝐶 propagates variable 𝑥 if setting 𝑥 to “wrong value” would make 𝐶 unsatisfiable
         E.g., if 𝑥 5 is false,
                                                      𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 + 5𝑥 5 ≥ 7
         propagates 𝑥 4 (since other coefficients do not add up to 7)




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                             The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    34/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT                Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




RUP Revisited

         Can define (reverse) unit propagation in a pseudo-Boolean setting [EGMN20]
         Constraint 𝐶 propagates variable 𝑥 if setting 𝑥 to “wrong value” would make 𝐶 unsatisfiable
         E.g., if 𝑥 5 is false,
                                                      𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 + 5𝑥 5 ≥ 7
         propagates 𝑥 4 (since other coefficients do not add up to 7)

         Risk for confusion!
              Constraint programming people might call this (reverse) integer bounds consistency
                        Does the same thing if we’re working with clauses
                        More interesting for general pseudo-Boolean constraints
                SAT people beware: constraints can propagate multiple times and multiple variables


Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                             The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    34/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT                      Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                 Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half

Propagation, Conflict, and Slack
                                                                                                   Í
         Slack measures how far assignment 𝜌 is from falsifying                                         𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ 𝐴
         Assuming normalised form:
                                                              Í                                  ∑︁
                                                      slack       𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ 𝐴; 𝜌           =               𝑎𝑖 − 𝐴
                                                                                               𝑖 : 𝜌 (ℓ𝑖 )≠0


         Consider 𝐶  𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 + 5𝑥 5 ≥ 7

                                       𝜌                slack(𝐶; 𝜌)               comment
                                      {}                          8

                                  {𝑥 5, 𝑥 4 }                     3               propagation doesn’t change slack
                              {𝑥 5, 𝑥 4, 𝑥 3, 𝑥 2 }             −2                conflict (slack < 0)

Propagation, Conflict, and Slack
                                                                                                   Í
         Slack measures how far assignment 𝜌 is from falsifying                                         𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ 𝐴
         Assuming normalised form:
                                                              Í                                  ∑︁
                                                      slack       𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ 𝐴; 𝜌           =               𝑎𝑖 − 𝐴
                                                                                               𝑖 : 𝜌 (ℓ𝑖 )≠0


         Consider 𝐶  𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 + 5𝑥 5 ≥ 7

                                       𝜌                slack(𝐶; 𝜌)               comment
                                      {}                          8
                                    {𝑥 5 }                        3               propagates 𝑥 4 (coefficient > slack)
                                  {𝑥 5, 𝑥 4 }                     3               propagation doesn’t change slack
                              {𝑥 5, 𝑥 4, 𝑥 3, 𝑥 2 }             −2                conflict (slack < 0)

         Note: constraint can be conflicting though not all variables assigned

Extension Variables, Part 2


         Suppose we want new, fresh reified variable 𝑟 encoding

                                                      𝑟 ⇔ (3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3)

         This time, introduce constraints

                                         3𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3                 5𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 5

         Again, needs support from the proof system in the form of strengthening rules

Proof Logs for “Cutting Planes with Strengthening”

         For satisfiable instances: just specify a satisfying assignment.
         For unsatisfiability: a sequence of pseudo-Boolean constraints in (slight extension of)
         OPB format [RM16]
                Each constraint follows “obviously” from what is known so far
                Either implicitly, by RUP. . .
                Or by an explicit cutting planes derivation. . .
                Or as an extension variable reifying a new constraint∗
                Final constraint is 0 ≥ 1




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    38/128
Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT               Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half

Optimisation and Enumeration Problems

         Optimisation
                                                      Í
                Define an objective 𝑓 =               𝑖 𝑤 𝑖 ℓ𝑖 , 𝑤 𝑖 ∈ Z, to minimise subject to the contraints in the formula
                To maximise, negate objective
                Log solution 𝛼 with soli rule ⇒ objective function value UB =
                                                                                                     Í
                                                                                                     𝑖 𝑤 𝑖 𝛼 (ℓ𝑖 )
                                                  Í
                Add objective-improving constraint 𝑖 𝑤𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≤ UB − 1
                Semantics for proof of optimality: “infeasible to find better solution than best so far”
                                                                                  Í
                Can also derive and certify (potentially non-tight) lower bound 𝑖 𝑤𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ LB

         Enumeration
                When a solution is found, can log it with solx rule
                Introduces a new constraint saying “not this solution”
                So the proof semantics is “infeasible, except for all the solutions I told you about”

Deleting Constraints



         In practice, important to erase constraints to save memory and time during verification
         Unsatisfiability proofs: fairly straightforward to deal with from point of view of proof logging
         Optimisation and enumeration proofs: significantly more delicate
         We will mostly ignore deletions during this tutorial for simplicity and clarity

                just do proof logging [basically: add print statements to solver code]

Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging — How and Why?

         If problem is (special case of) 0-1 integer linear program
                just do proof logging [basically: add print statements to solver code]
         Otherwise
                do trusted or verified translation to 0-1 ILP
                do proof logging for 0-1 ILP formulation [but solver still works with original input]

Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging — How and Why?

         If problem is (special case of) 0-1 integer linear program
                   just do proof logging [basically: add print statements to solver code]
         Otherwise
                   do trusted or verified translation to 0-1 ILP
                   do proof logging for 0-1 ILP formulation [but solver still works with original input]

         Goldilocks compromise between expressivity and simplicity:
          1 0-1 ILP expressive formalism for combinatorial problems (including objective)

               2   Powerful reasoning capturing many combinatorial arguments
               3   Efficient reification using big-M constraints

         Otherwise

Strengthening Rules (And the Truth About Extension Variables)

Strengthening Rules (And the Truth About Extension Variables)

         When is it allowed to derive a new constraint? If it is (clear that it is) implied?
         Sometimes weaker criterion needed — recall that to get variable 𝑟 encoding

                                                      𝑟 ⇔ (3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3)

         we introduced pseudo-Boolean constraints

                                         3𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3                 5𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 5

         Cutting planes method inherently cannot certify such constraints — they are not implied!

Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging        Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




Strengthening Rules (And the Truth About Extension Variables)

         When is it allowed to derive a new constraint? If it is (clear that it is) implied?
         Sometimes weaker criterion needed — recall that to get variable 𝑟 encoding

                                                      𝑟 ⇔ (3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3)

         we introduced pseudo-Boolean constraints

                                         3𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3                 5𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 5

         Cutting planes method inherently cannot certify such constraints — they are not implied!
         Wish to allow without-loss-of-generality arguments that can derive non-implied constraints

Redundance-Based Strengthening



Redundance-Based Strengthening
        𝐶 is “redundant” with respect to 𝐹 if 𝐹 and 𝐹 ∪ {𝐶} are equisatisfiable
         [apologies for the terminology — this is inherited from SAT proof logging]




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                              The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    44/128
Introduction                     Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half

Redundance-Based Strengthening



Redundance-Based Strengthening
        𝐶 is “redundant” with respect to 𝐹 if 𝐹 and 𝐹 ∪ {𝐶} are equisatisfiable
         [apologies for the terminology — this is inherited from SAT proof logging]

         Redundance-based strengthening [BT19, GN21] (extending RAT rule of SAT proof logging)
        𝐶 is redundant with respect to 𝐹 if and only if there is a substitution 𝜔 (mapping variables to truth
        values or literals), called a witness, for which

                                                         𝐹 ∪ {¬𝐶} |= (𝐹 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔

Redundance-Based Strengthening
        𝐶 is “redundant” with respect to 𝐹 if 𝐹 and 𝐹 ∪ {𝐶} are equisatisfiable
         [apologies for the terminology — this is inherited from SAT proof logging]

         Redundance-based strengthening [BT19, GN21] (extending RAT rule of SAT proof logging)
        𝐶 is redundant with respect to 𝐹 if and only if there is a substitution 𝜔 (mapping variables to truth
        values or literals), called a witness, for which

                                                         𝐹 ∪ {¬𝐶} |= (𝐹 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔

         Proof sketch for interesting direction:
             If 𝛼 satisfies 𝐹 but falsifies 𝐶, then 𝛼 satisfies (𝐹 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔 [notation means apply 𝜔 and simplify]
             I.e., 𝛼 ◦ 𝜔 satisfies 𝐹 ∪ {𝐶}
         Witness 𝜔 should be specified, and implication needs to be efficiently verifiable — every
         𝐷 ∈ (𝐹 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔 should follow from 𝐹 ∪ {¬𝐶} either
           1 “obviously” (e.g., by reverse unit propagation) or

         [apologies for the terminology — this is inherited from SAT proof logging]

         Redundance-based strengthening [BT19, GN21] (extending RAT rule of SAT proof logging)
        𝐶 is redundant with respect to 𝐹 if and only if there is a substitution 𝜔 (mapping variables to truth
        values or literals), called a witness, for which

             If 𝛼 satisfies 𝐹 but falsifies 𝐶, then 𝛼 satisfies (𝐹 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔 [notation means apply 𝜔 and simplify]
             I.e., 𝛼 ◦ 𝜔 satisfies 𝐹 ∪ {𝐶}
         Witness 𝜔 should be specified, and implication needs to be efficiently verifiable — every
         𝐷 ∈ (𝐹 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔 should follow from 𝐹 ∪ {¬𝐶} either
           1 “obviously” (e.g., by reverse unit propagation) or
           2 by explicitly presented derivation

Deriving 𝑟 ⇔ (3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3) Using the Redundance Rule
         Want to derive
                                            3𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3                 5𝑎 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 5
         using condition 𝐹 ∪ {¬𝐶} |= (𝐹 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔
                                                                                      
               1   𝐹 ∪ {¬(3𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3)} |= 𝐹 ∪ {3𝑟 + 3𝑥 + 2𝑦 + 𝑧 + 𝑤 ≥ 3} ↾𝜔

         VeriPB code:
                                               red 3 ∼r 3 x 2 y 1 z 1 w >= 3 : r -> 0 ;
                                               red 5 r 3 ∼x 2 ∼y 1 ∼z 1 ∼w >= 5 : r -> 1 ;

                                                             𝐹 ∪ {¬𝐶} |= (𝐹 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 ≤ 𝑓 }

Redundance-Based and Dominance-Based Strengthening for Optimisation



Soundness of Dominance Rule
         Dominance-based strengthening (simplified)
         Add constraint 𝐶 to formula 𝐹 if exists witness substitution 𝜔 s.t.
                                                                      𝐹 ∪ {¬𝐶} |= 𝐹↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 < 𝑓 }

         Why is this sound?
          1 Suppose 𝛼 satisfies 𝐹 but falsifies 𝐶 (i.e., satisfies ¬𝐶)

          2 Then 𝛼 ◦ 𝜔 satisfies 𝐹 and 𝑓 (𝛼 ◦ 𝜔) < 𝑓 (𝛼)

Soundness of Dominance Rule
         Dominance-based strengthening (simplified)
         Add constraint 𝐶 to formula 𝐹 if exists witness substitution 𝜔 s.t.
                                                                      𝐹 ∪ {¬𝐶} |= 𝐹↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 < 𝑓 }

         Why is this sound?
          1 Suppose 𝛼 satisfies 𝐹 but falsifies 𝐶 (i.e., satisfies ¬𝐶)

          2 Then 𝛼 ◦ 𝜔 satisfies 𝐹 and 𝑓 (𝛼 ◦ 𝜔) < 𝑓 (𝛼)

          3 If 𝛼 ◦ 𝜔 satisfies 𝐶, we’re done
                                                                           
          4 Otherwise (𝛼 ◦ 𝜔) ◦ 𝜔 satisfies 𝐹 and 𝑓 (𝛼 ◦ 𝜔) ◦ 𝜔 < 𝑓 𝛼 ◦ 𝜔

          5 If (𝛼 ◦ 𝜔) ◦ 𝜔 satisfies 𝐶, we’re done
                                                                                        
          6 Otherwise (𝛼 ◦ 𝜔) ◦ 𝜔 ◦ 𝜔 satisfies 𝐹 and 𝑓 ((𝛼 ◦ 𝜔) ◦ 𝜔) ◦ 𝜔 < 𝑓 (𝛼 ◦ 𝜔) ◦ 𝜔

          7 ...
                                                     ′
          8 Can’t go on forever, so finally reach 𝛼 satisfying both 𝐹 and 𝐶

Redundance-Based and Dominance-Based Strengthening for Optimisation



Strength of Dominance Rule
         Dominance-based strengthening (stronger, still simplified)
         If 𝐶 1, 𝐶 2, . . . , 𝐶𝑚−1 have been derived from 𝐹 (maybe using dominance), then can derive 𝐶𝑚 if exists
         witness substitution 𝜔 s.t.
                                                  𝐹 ∪ {𝐶 1, . . . , 𝐶𝑚−1 } ∪ {¬𝐶𝑚 } |= 𝐹↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 < 𝑓 }
         Only consider 𝐹 — no need to show that any 𝐶𝑖 ↾𝜔 implied!




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                         ACP Summer School ’26    48/128
Introduction                         Proof Logging for SAT                       Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half

Redundance-Based and Dominance-Based Strengthening for Optimisation



Strength of Dominance Rule
         Dominance-based strengthening (stronger, still simplified)
         If 𝐶 1, 𝐶 2, . . . , 𝐶𝑚−1 have been derived from 𝐹 (maybe using dominance), then can derive 𝐶𝑚 if exists
         witness substitution 𝜔 s.t.
                                                  𝐹 ∪ {𝐶 1, . . . , 𝐶𝑚−1 } ∪ {¬𝐶𝑚 } |= 𝐹↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 < 𝑓 }
         Only consider 𝐹 — no need to show that any 𝐶𝑖 ↾𝜔 implied!
         Now why is this sound?
            Same inductive proof as before, but nested

Redundance-Based and Dominance-Based Strengthening for Optimisation



Strength of Dominance Rule
         Dominance-based strengthening (stronger, still simplified)
         If 𝐶 1, 𝐶 2, . . . , 𝐶𝑚−1 have been derived from 𝐹 (maybe using dominance), then can derive 𝐶𝑚 if exists
         witness substitution 𝜔 s.t.
                                                  𝐹 ∪ {𝐶 1, . . . , 𝐶𝑚−1 } ∪ {¬𝐶𝑚 } |= 𝐹↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 < 𝑓 }
         Only consider 𝐹 — no need to show that any 𝐶𝑖 ↾𝜔 implied!
         Now why is this sound?
            Same inductive proof as before, but nested
            Or pick solution 𝛼 satisfying 𝐹 and minimizing 𝑓 and argue by contradiction
         Further extensions:
             Define dominance rule w.r.t. order independent of objective
             Switch between different orders in same proof
             See [BGMN23, ABB+ 26b] fore more details

Strengthening Rules: Syntax
         red ⟨Constraint 𝐶⟩ : ⟨var1⟩ -> ⟨val1⟩ . . . ⟨varN⟩ -> ⟨valN⟩ : subproof

         qed;

                 Witness 𝜔 should be explicitly specified in proof log
                 Subproofs of proof goals should also be explicit
                 Except can be skipped for proof goals that “obviously” follow, e.g.,
                          by reverse unit propagation (RUP)
                          by simple syntactic implication from other constraint (as in our reification example)
                          if the proof goal is not affected by the witness substitution 𝜔

Successful Applications of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging with VeriPB
         Surprisingly, pseudo-Boolean reasoning with strengthening rules sufficient to efficiently certify
         wide range of combinatorial solving techniques and paradigms:

Introduction                  Proof Logging for SAT              Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging   Strengthening Rules                    End of First Half




Successful Applications of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging with VeriPB
         Surprisingly, pseudo-Boolean reasoning with strengthening rules sufficient to efficiently certify
         wide range of combinatorial solving techniques and paradigms:
           1 Boolean satisfiability (SAT) solving including advanced techniques such as
                        Gaussian elimination [GN21]
                        symmetry breaking [BGMN23, ABB+ 26b]
               2   SAT-based optimization (MaxSAT) [VDB22, BBN+ 23, BBN+ 24, IOT+ 24, VCB26]
               3   Pseudo-Boolean solving and optimization [GMNO22, KLM+ 25]
               4   Graph solving [GMN20, GMM+ 20, GMM+ 24, DKK+ 26]
               5   Dynamic programming and decision diagrams [DMM+ 24]
               6   Presolving in 0–1 integer linear programming [HOGN24]
               7   Constraint programming [EGMN20, GMN22, MM23, MMN24, MM25]
               8   Automated planning [DHN+ 25]
               9   Enumeration of solutions/models [MNOT26]

Summing up the First Lecture
                Combinatorial solving and optimisation is a true success story

                But ensuring correctness is a crucial, and not yet satisfactorily addressed, concern

                Certifying solvers producing machine-verifiable proofs of correctness seems like most
                promising approach

                Cutting planes reasoning with pseudo-Boolean constraints seems to hit a sweet spot between
                simplicity and expressivity

Implementation and Performance

        Proof logging implementation
                 Don’t change solver
                 Just add proof logging statements (plus some book-keeping)
                 Proof generation should be very efficient


        Performance goals for proof checking
        Want linear(ish) scaling in terms of original solver running time for
                 proof size
                 proof checking time

VeriPB Proof Structure: Syntax

    pseudo-Boolean proof version 3.0
    f ⟨M⟩ ;
    ⟨derivation part⟩
    output ⟨output part⟩
    conclusion ⟨conclusion part⟩
    end pseudo-Boolean proof ;

Strengthening Rules in Their (Almost) Full Formal Glory

        Witness 𝜔: substitution mapping variables to truth values or literals

        Redundance-based strengthening (witness 𝜔 show how to “patch assignment”)
        Derive constraint 𝐶 from 𝒞 ∪ 𝒟 if exists witness 𝜔 such that

                                                                                        𝑧↾𝜔 , 𝑧®)
                                       𝒞 ∪ 𝒟 ∪ {¬𝐶} ⊢ (𝒞 ∪ 𝒟 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 ≤ 𝑓 } ∪ 𝒪(®

Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




Strengthening Rules in Their (Almost) Full Formal Glory

        Witness 𝜔: substitution mapping variables to truth values or literals

        Redundance-based strengthening (witness 𝜔 show how to “patch assignment”)
        Derive constraint 𝐶 from 𝒞 ∪ 𝒟 if exists witness 𝜔 such that

                                                                                        𝑧↾𝜔 , 𝑧®)
                                       𝒞 ∪ 𝒟 ∪ {¬𝐶} ⊢ (𝒞 ∪ 𝒟 ∪ {𝐶})↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 ≤ 𝑓 } ∪ 𝒪(®


        Dominance-based strengthening (witness 𝜔 “drives down potential”)
        Derive constraint 𝐶 from 𝒞 ∪ 𝒟 if exists witness 𝜔 such that

                                                                            𝑧↾𝜔 , 𝑧®) ∪ ¬𝒪(®
                                       𝒞 ∪ 𝒟 ∪ {¬𝐶} ⊢ 𝒞↾𝜔 ∪ {𝑓 ↾𝜔 ≤ 𝑓 } ∪ 𝒪(®              𝑧, 𝑧®↾𝜔 )

Syntax for Strengthening Rules

        red ⟨Constraint 𝐶⟩ : ⟨var1⟩ -> ⟨val1⟩ . . . ⟨varN⟩ -> ⟨valN⟩ : subproof
             subproofs for proof goals
        qed;

        dom ⟨Constraint 𝐶⟩ : ⟨var1⟩ -> ⟨val1⟩ . . . ⟨varN⟩ -> ⟨valN⟩ : subproof
             subproofs for proof goals
        qed;

Syntax for Strengthening Rules

        red ⟨Constraint 𝐶⟩ : ⟨var1⟩ -> ⟨val1⟩ . . . ⟨varN⟩ -> ⟨valN⟩ : subproof
             subproofs for proof goals
        qed;

        dom ⟨Constraint 𝐶⟩ : ⟨var1⟩ -> ⟨val1⟩ . . . ⟨varN⟩ -> ⟨valN⟩ : subproof
             subproofs for proof goals
        qed;

                 Witness 𝜔 should be explicitly specified in proof log
                 Subproofs of proof goals should also be explicit
                 But can be skipped for proof goals “obvious” to proof checker

The Problem of Deleting Constraints

        Important to allow deletions of constraints from database
                 Improves practical performance
                 Affects power of strengthening rules

Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




The Problem of Deleting Constraints

        Important to allow deletions of constraints from database
                 Improves practical performance
                 Affects power of strengthening rules

        But powerful strengthening rules create problems:
                 Unsatisfiable formulas can turn satisfiable
                 Satisfiable formulas can turn unsatisfiable(!)




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   63/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




The Problem of Deleting Constraints

        Important to allow deletions of constraints from database
                 Improves practical performance
                 Affects power of strengthening rules

        But powerful strengthening rules create problems:
                 Unsatisfiable formulas can turn satisfiable
                 Satisfiable formulas can turn unsatisfiable(!)

        Optimisation: deletion can create too-good-to-be-true solutions
        Enumeration: deletion can reintroduce already enumerated solutions




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   63/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




The Problem of Deleting Constraints

        Important to allow deletions of constraints from database
                 Improves practical performance
                 Affects power of strengthening rules

        But powerful strengthening rules create problems:
                 Unsatisfiable formulas can turn satisfiable
                 Satisfiable formulas can turn unsatisfiable(!)

        Optimisation: deletion can create too-good-to-be-true solutions
        Enumeration: deletion can reintroduce already enumerated solutions
        Solution: distinguish between deletion from core set 𝒞 and derived set 𝒟


Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   63/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




Deletion, Core Transfer, and Order Change

        Deletion
         1 Deletion of constraint 𝐶 always OK from derived set 𝒟

             2   OK from core set 𝒞 only if 𝐶 can be rederived from 𝒞 \ {𝐶} with redundance rule
                 (otherwise unchecked deletion — special conditions apply)




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   64/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




Deletion, Core Transfer, and Order Change

        Deletion
         1 Deletion of constraint 𝐶 always OK from derived set 𝒟

             2   OK from core set 𝒞 only if 𝐶 can be rederived from 𝒞 \ {𝐶} with redundance rule
                 (otherwise unchecked deletion — special conditions apply)
        Similar to deletion rule in [JHB12] (but not implemented in DRAT)




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   64/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




Deletion, Core Transfer, and Order Change

        Deletion
         1 Deletion of constraint 𝐶 always OK from derived set 𝒟

             2   OK from core set 𝒞 only if 𝐶 can be rederived from 𝒞 \ {𝐶} with redundance rule
                 (otherwise unchecked deletion — special conditions apply)
        Similar to deletion rule in [JHB12] (but not implemented in DRAT)

        Core transfer
        Constraints from 𝒟 can be moved to 𝒞




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   64/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




Deletion, Core Transfer, and Order Change

        Deletion
         1 Deletion of constraint 𝐶 always OK from derived set 𝒟

             2   OK from core set 𝒞 only if 𝐶 can be rederived from 𝒞 \ {𝐶} with redundance rule
                 (otherwise unchecked deletion — special conditions apply)
        Similar to deletion rule in [JHB12] (but not implemented in DRAT)

        Core transfer
        Constraints from 𝒟 can be moved to 𝒞

        Change of order
        Possible to change order only if 𝒟 = ∅

Conclusions for Decision Problems

        NONE
        Status is undetermined
        SAT [ : ⟨assignment⟩]
        Check provided assignmed against original formula
        If no assignment given, then
                 solution should have been logged
                 no unchecked deletion must have occurred

        UNSAT [ : ⟨constraint ID⟩]
        Only valid if no solution has been logged
        Check that specified constraint is contradictory (technically: negative slack)
        If no constraint specified, check that database contains contradictory constraint

Optimization Problems

        Any solution 𝛼 found is logged with soli “log solution and improve” command
                 provided solution 𝛼 checked against current core set 𝒞
                                                   Í           Í
                 Objective-improving constraint 𝑖 𝑤𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≤ −1 + 𝑖 𝑤𝑖 · 𝛼 (ℓ𝑖 ) added to core set
                 (forces search for better solutions)

Optimization Problems

                 provided solution 𝛼 checked against current core set 𝒞
                                                   Í           Í
                 Objective-improving constraint 𝑖 𝑤𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≤ −1 + 𝑖 𝑤𝑖 · 𝛼 (ℓ𝑖 ) added to core set
                 (forces search for better solutions)

        Note that
                 𝛼 need not be solution for original formula
                 but such solution can be reconstructed from the proof

Optimization Problems

        Any solution 𝛼 found is logged with soli “log solution and improve” command
                 provided solution 𝛼 checked against current core set 𝒞
                                                   Í           Í
                 Objective-improving constraint 𝑖 𝑤𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≤ −1 + 𝑖 𝑤𝑖 · 𝛼 (ℓ𝑖 ) added to core set
                 (forces search for better solutions)

        Note that
                 𝛼 need not be solution for original formula
                 but such solution can be reconstructed from the proof

        Proof of optimality: Contradiction derived from objective-improving constraint
        Proof format supports not just optimality, but also non-tight upper and lower bounds

Conclusions for Optimization Problems
        NONE
        No solution or lower bound found
        BOUNDS ⟨LB⟩ [ : ⟨constraint ID⟩ ] ⟨UB⟩ [ : ⟨assignment⟩ ]
        ⟨LB⟩ and ⟨UB⟩ are integers or inf; optimality if ⟨LB⟩ = ⟨UB⟩

Conclusions for Optimization Problems
        NONE
        No solution or lower bound found
        BOUNDS ⟨LB⟩ [ : ⟨constraint ID⟩ ] ⟨UB⟩ [ : ⟨assignment⟩ ]
        ⟨LB⟩ and ⟨UB⟩ are integers or inf; optimality if ⟨LB⟩ = ⟨UB⟩

        Lower bound
        Constraint ⟨constraint ID⟩, if specified, should imply lower bound
        Otherwise, 𝑓 ≥ ⟨LB⟩ should be “obvious” to proof checker from current database

        No solution or lower bound found
        BOUNDS ⟨LB⟩ [ : ⟨constraint ID⟩ ] ⟨UB⟩ [ : ⟨assignment⟩ ]
        ⟨LB⟩ and ⟨UB⟩ are integers or inf; optimality if ⟨LB⟩ = ⟨UB⟩

        Lower bound
        Constraint ⟨constraint ID⟩, if specified, should imply lower bound
        Otherwise, 𝑓 ≥ ⟨LB⟩ should be “obvious” to proof checker from current database
        Upper bound
        Check provided assignment against original formula
        If no assignment specified, then
                 solution with value ⟨UB⟩ should have been logged
                 no unchecked deletion must have occurred

Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   67/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




More About VeriPB Proof Syntax
        Variables
                 start with a letter in A-Z or a-z
                 continue with characters in A-Z, a-z, 0-9, or []{}-_ˆ
                 (square and curly brackets, hyphen, underscore, and caret)
                 contain at least two characters

        Constraints
        Are referred to by positive integers (constraint IDs)
        Derivation rules and requirements
        Come in two flavours
             1   kernel format for formally verified proof checker
             2   augmented format with convenience rules such as reverse unit propagation (RUP)

        See https://VeriPB.org for more details
Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   68/128

Syntactic Sugar: Labels for Constraints
        Constraint identifiers are consecutive numbers 1, 2, 3, . . .
                 Can be annoying to keep track of the exact numbers when writing proofs
                 Or plain difficult if several (sub)solvers collaborate
        Labels simplify things — just aliases @⟨label⟩ for constraint IDs (in input file or proof file)

            * input file (.opb)                                                                               % proof file (.pbp)
            @foo 1 x1 1 x2 >= 1 ;                                                                             @sum pol @foo @bar + @baz + ;
            @bar 1 x1 1 x3 >= 1 ;                                                                             pol @sum 2 d ;
            @baz 1 x1 1 x3 >= 1 ;

Syntactic Sugar: Labels for Constraints
        Constraint identifiers are consecutive numbers 1, 2, 3, . . .
                 Can be annoying to keep track of the exact numbers when writing proofs
                 Or plain difficult if several (sub)solvers collaborate
        Labels simplify things — just aliases @⟨label⟩ for constraint IDs (in input file or proof file)

            * input file (.opb)                                                                               % proof file (.pbp)
            @foo 1 x1 1 x2 >= 1 ;                                                                             @sum pol @foo @bar + @baz + ;
            @bar 1 x1 1 x3 >= 1 ;                                                                             pol @sum 2 d ;
            @baz 1 x1 1 x3 >= 1 ;

                 Can be used anywhere a constraint ID is expected
                 Prefixing of proof rule assigns label to derived constraint
                 Defining a label again just overwrites it — can be convenient when referring to simplified
                 form of “the same constraint”

Linear Pseudo-Boolean Constraints and Normalised Form
        Usually, pseudo-Boolean constraints are any 0–1 integer linear constraints on the form
                                                  Í
                                                   𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ⊲⊳ 𝐴

                 ⊲⊳ ∈ {≥, ≤, =, >, <}
                 𝑎𝑖 , 𝐴 ∈ Z
                 literals ℓ𝑖 : 𝑥𝑖 or 𝑥 𝑖 (where 𝑥𝑖 + 𝑥 𝑖 = 1)
                 variables 𝑥𝑖 take values 0 = false or 1 = true

Linear Pseudo-Boolean Constraints and Normalised Form
        Usually, pseudo-Boolean constraints are any 0–1 integer linear constraints on the form
                                                  Í
                                                   𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ⊲⊳ 𝐴

                 ⊲⊳ ∈ {≥, ≤, =, >, <}
                 𝑎𝑖 , 𝐴 ∈ Z
                 literals ℓ𝑖 : 𝑥𝑖 or 𝑥 𝑖 (where 𝑥𝑖 + 𝑥 𝑖 = 1)
                 variables 𝑥𝑖 take values 0 = false or 1 = true

        For us, convenient to use normalised form [Bar95] (without loss of generality)
                                                  Í
                                                    𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ 𝐴

                 constraint always greater-than-or-equal
                 𝑎𝑖 , 𝐴 ∈ N non-negative
                           Í
                 𝐴 = deg( 𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ𝑖 ≥ 𝐴) referred to as degree (of falsity)

Conversion to Normalised Form: Example
        Normalised form is used for convenience and without loss of generality
                                                               −𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 − 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 − 5𝑥 5 < 0
             1   Make inequality non-strict
                                                                  −𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 − 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 − 5𝑥 5 ≤ −1
             2   Multiply by −1 to get greater-than-or-equal
                                                                    𝑥 1 − 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 − 4𝑥 4 + 5𝑥 5 ≥ 1

                                                                                                                𝑖 𝑎𝑖 ℓ 𝑖 ≥ 𝑊 − 𝐴
        In what follows:
            Use syntactic sugar when convenient
            Assume (implicit) normalisation whenever it matters
            Write  for syntactic equality

More Syntactic Sugar: Reification in VeriPB Syntax
        For big-M reification constraints, can be annoying to compute the right constant
        So VeriPB allows to write reified constraints with arrows

                                                                    r ==> ⟨constraint⟩ ;
                                                                    r <== ⟨constraint⟩ ;
                                                                    r <==> ⟨constraint⟩ ;

More Syntactic Sugar: Reification in VeriPB Syntax
        For big-M reification constraints, can be annoying to compute the right constant
        So VeriPB allows to write reified constraints with arrows

                                                                    r ==> ⟨constraint⟩ ;
                                                                    r <== ⟨constraint⟩ ;
                                                                    r <==> ⟨constraint⟩ ;

                 <==> stands for two constraints and lives in the input file only
                 <== and <==> take exactly one literal on the left
                 ==> allows one or more literals, read as their conjunction

                                                                    r ==> ⟨constraint⟩ ;

             ==> allows one or more literals, read as their conjunction
                 Í

        For 𝐶  𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 ≥ 5 we have
             deg(𝐶) = 5
                 Í
             𝑊 = 𝑖 𝑎𝑖 = 10

                      Syntactic sugar                                                                                    Meaning
                               r ==>             1 x1 2 x2 3 x3 4 x4 >= 5 ;                                               5𝑟 + 𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 ≥ 5
                               r <==             1 x1 2 x2 3 x3 4 x4 >= 5 ;                                              6𝑟 + 𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 ≥ 6
                               r <==> 1 x1 2 x2 3 x3 4 x4 >= 5 ;                                                 1st and 2nd constraint, in that order
            r1 ∼r2 r3 ==>                        1 x1 2 x2 3 x3 4 x4 >= 5 ;                               5𝑟 1 + 5𝑟 2 + 5𝑟 3 + 𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 ≥ 5

                 Right reification: negate reification literal(s) and add with coefficient deg(𝐶) = 5
                 Left reification: negate constraint and add reification literal with coefficient deg(¬𝐶) = 6
Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                            The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                                 ACP Summer School ’26   76/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up




Calculations with Reified Constraints: Concrete Examples
        Still with 𝐶  𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 ≥ 5, so that ¬𝐶  𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 ≥ 6
        (and recall that ℓ + ℓ = 1, so that opposing literals cancel and leave 1)

                                                𝐶 + ¬𝐶  (𝑥 1 + 𝑥 1 ) + 2(𝑥 2 + 𝑥 2 ) + 3(𝑥 3 + 𝑥 3 ) + 4(𝑥 4 + 𝑥 4 ) ≥ 11
                                                                 0 ≥ 11 − 10
                                                                 0≥1
                 deg(𝐶) · (𝑟 ≥ 1) + (𝑟 ⇒ 𝐶)  5(𝑟 + 𝑟 ) + 𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 ≥ 10
                                                                 𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 3 + 4𝑥 4 ≥ 10 − 5
                                                                 𝐶
                                         𝐶 + (𝑟 ⇐ 𝐶)  6𝑟 + (𝑥 1 + 𝑥 1 ) + 2(𝑥 2 + 𝑥 2 ) + 3(𝑥 3 + 𝑥 3 ) + 4(𝑥 4 + 𝑥 4 ) ≥ 11
                                                                 6𝑟 ≥ 11 − 10
                                                                 deg(¬𝐶) · 𝑟 ≥ 1

        Suppose we have the constraints

                                                      𝑟2 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥3 ≥ 2                     ⇝ 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥3 ≥ 2

        Assume 𝑟 1 = 0 and 𝑟 2 = 1 and simplify — then cutting planes can derive contradiction
                                           𝑥1 + 𝑥2 ≥ 1               𝑥1 + 𝑥3 ≥ 1
                                  Add

                                                   Divide by 2

                                                             Add
                                                                                                            0≥1
         This means that the original constraints imply 𝑟 1 + 𝑟 2 ≥ 1

The Same Derivation Without Assumptions
        Now forget about the assumptions and run exactly the same derivation on the original constraints:
                                𝑟1 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 ≥ 1                    𝑟1 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥3 ≥ 1
                       Add
                                               2𝑟 1 + 2𝑥 1 + 𝑥 2 + 𝑥 3 ≥ 2                               𝑥2 + 𝑥3 ≥ 1
                                   Add
                                                                 2𝑟 1 + 2𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 2𝑥 3 ≥ 3
                                               Divide by 2

The Same Derivation Without Assumptions
        Now forget about the assumptions and run exactly the same derivation on the original constraints:
                                𝑟1 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 ≥ 1                    𝑟1 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥3 ≥ 1
                       Add
                                               2𝑟 1 + 2𝑥 1 + 𝑥 2 + 𝑥 3 ≥ 2                               𝑥2 + 𝑥3 ≥ 1
                                   Add
                                                                 2𝑟 1 + 2𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 2 + 2𝑥 3 ≥ 3
                                               Divide by 2
                                                                   𝑟1 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥3 ≥ 2                                    𝑟2 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 + 𝑥3 ≥ 2
                                                           Add
                                                                                                             𝑟1 + 𝑟 2 ≥ 1
        This yields

                                                𝑟1 + 𝑟 2 ≥ 1                         [VeriPB syntax: 1 r1 1 ∼r2 >= 1 ; ]

        or equivalently

                                 𝑟 1 ∧ 𝑟 2 ⇒ (0 ≥ 1)                                 [VeriPB syntax: ∼r1 r2 ==> >= 1 ; ]

The Same Derivation Without Assumptions
        Now forget about the assumptions and run exactly the same derivation on the original constraints:
                                𝑟1 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥2 ≥ 1                    𝑟1 + 𝑥1 + 𝑥3 ≥ 1
                       Add
                                               2𝑟 1 + 2𝑥 1 + 𝑥 2 + 𝑥 3 ≥ 2                               𝑥2 + 𝑥3 ≥ 1

        or equivalently

                                 𝑟 1 ∧ 𝑟 2 ⇒ (0 ≥ 1)                                 [VeriPB syntax: ∼r1 r2 ==> >= 1 ; ]

        That is, our assumptions imply contradiction

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Maximum Clique Solvers



        There are a lot of dedicated solvers for clique problems
        But there are issues:
            “State-of-the-art” solvers have been buggy.
                 Often undetected: error rate of around 0.1 % [MPP19]

        Often used inside other solvers
                 An off-by-one result can cause much larger errors

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



A Brief and Incomplete Guide to Clique Solving (1/4)



        Recursive maximum clique algorithm:
                 Pick a vertex 𝑣
                 Either 𝑣 is in the clique. . .
                          Throw away every vertex not adjacent to 𝑣
                          If vertices remain, recurse
                 . . . or 𝑣 is not in the clique
                          Throw away 𝑣 (and all incident edges) and pick another vertex

        Key data structures:
                 Growing clique 𝐶
                 Shrinking set of potential vertices 𝑃
                          All the vertices we haven’t thrown away yet
                          Every 𝑣 ∈ 𝑃 is adjacent to every 𝑤 ∈ 𝐶

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



A Brief and Incomplete Guide to Clique Solving (2/4)


        Key data structures:
                 Growing clique 𝐶
                 Shrinking set of potential vertices 𝑃
                          All the vertices we haven’t thrown away yet
                          Every 𝑣 ∈ 𝑃 is adjacent to every 𝑤 ∈ 𝐶

        Branch and bound:
                 Remember the biggest clique 𝐶 ★ found so far
                 If |𝐶 | + |𝑃 | ≤ 𝐶 ★ , no need to keep going

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Making a Certifying Clique Solver


             1   Output a pseudo-Boolean encoding of the problem
                          Clique problems have several standard file formats
                          Give the constraints labels, so the proof can refer to them by name

             2   Make solver generate proof log for search tree
                          Output a small header
                          Output something on every backtrack
                          Output something every time a solution is found
                          Output a small footer

             3   Figure out how to certify the bound function

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



A Pseudo-Boolean Encoding for Clique (in OPB Format)
                                                                                                 12
                                                                                        11                 1

                                                                               10                                 2


                                                                           9                                           3


                                                                               8                                  4

                                                                                         7                 5
                                                                                                 6
        * #variable= 12 #constraint= 41
        min: 1 ~x1 1 ~x2 1 ~x3 1 ~x4 . . . and so on. . . 1 ~x11 1 ~x12 ;
        @noedge1_3 -1 x3 -1 x1 >= -1 ;
        @noedge2_3 -1 x3 -1 x2 >= -1 ;
        @noedge1_4 -1 x4 -1 x1 >= -1 ;
        * . . . and a further 38 similar lines for the remaining non-edges

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



A Pseudo-Boolean Encoding for Clique (in OPB Format)
                                                                                                 12
                                                                                        11                 1

                                                                               10                                 2


                                                                           9                                           3


                                                                               8                                  4

                                                                                         7                 5
                                                                                                 6
        * #variable= 12 #constraint= 41
        min: 1 ~x1 1 ~x2 1 ~x3 1 ~x4 . . . and so on. . . 1 ~x11 1 ~x12 ;
        @noedge1_3 -1 x3 -1 x1 >= -1 ;
        @noedge2_3 -1 x3 -1 x2 >= -1 ;
        @noedge1_4 -1 x4 -1 x1 >= -1 ;
        * . . . and a further 38 similar lines for the remaining non-edges
        𝑥𝑖 = 1 means “vertex 𝑖 is in the clique”
        VeriPB proof minimises number of vertices not selected, so 4-clique yields objective value 12 − 4 = 8

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



The Problem of Counting


        The proof checker isn’t smart enough to work out that
                 vertices 1, 3 and 9 can all be given the same colour. . .
                 and each colour class contributes at most one vertex to a clique. . .
                 so “find me a clique with more than three vertices” can’t be satisfied once we’ve accepted
                 both 10 and 11
                                                                                                                           11             1
        Unit propagation never sees this:
                                                                                                                      10                       2
                 The bound needs counting
                                                                                                                  9                                 3
                 But the encoding only ever talks about pairs of vertices
                                                                                                                      8                        4

                                                                                                                           7              5
                                                                                                                                  6

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Dealing With Colourings
        So the colour bound doesn’t follow by reverse unit propagation. . .
        But we can lazily recover at-most-one constraints for each colour class!

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Dealing With Colourings
        So the colour bound doesn’t follow by reverse unit propagation. . .
        But we can lazily recover at-most-one constraints for each colour class!

                                                 (𝑥 1 + 𝑥 6 ≥ 1)
                                             + (𝑥 1 + 𝑥 9 ≥ 1)                                              = 2𝑥 1 + 𝑥 6 + 𝑥 9 ≥ 2
                                             + (𝑥 6 + 𝑥 9 ≥ 1)                                              = 2𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 6 + 2𝑥 9 ≥ 3
                                                                /2                                          = 𝑥1 + 𝑥6 + 𝑥9 ≥ 2
                                                                                                          i.e. 𝑥 1 + 𝑥 6 + 𝑥 9 ≤ 1

Proof Logging Recap     VeriPB Proofs in Depth    Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms                       Constraint Programming    Wrapping up

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Dealing With Colourings
        So the colour bound doesn’t follow by reverse unit propagation. . .
        But we can lazily recover at-most-one constraints for each colour class!

                                                 (𝑥 1 + 𝑥 6 ≥ 1)
                                             + (𝑥 1 + 𝑥 9 ≥ 1)                                              = 2𝑥 1 + 𝑥 6 + 𝑥 9 ≥ 2
                                             + (𝑥 6 + 𝑥 9 ≥ 1)                                              = 2𝑥 1 + 2𝑥 6 + 2𝑥 9 ≥ 3
                                                                /2                                          = 𝑥1 + 𝑥6 + 𝑥9 ≥ 2
                                                                                                          i.e. 𝑥 1 + 𝑥 6 + 𝑥 9 ≤ 1

           This generalises to colour classes of any size 𝑣
                 Each non-edge is used exactly once, 𝑣 (𝑣 − 1) additions
                 𝑣 − 3 multiplications and 𝑣 − 2 divisions
        Solvers don’t need to “understand” cutting planes to write out this derivation
Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                            The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                               ACP Summer School ’26   93/128
Proof Logging Recap     VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Details on At-Most-One Constraint Recovery (3 Variables)
                                                                  
                 Derive constraint 𝑥 1 + . . . + 𝑥𝑛 ≤ 1 from 𝑛2 constraints 𝑥𝑖 + 𝑥 𝑗 ≤ 1
                 I.e., 𝑥 1 + . . . + 𝑥 𝑛 ≥ 𝑛 − 1 from constraints 𝑥 𝑖 + 𝑥 𝑗 ≥ 1 in normalised form

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



What The Revised Proof with Explicit Counting Looks Like
        pseudo-Boolean proof version 3.0
        @obj soli x7 x9 x12 ;
        rup 1 ~x12 1 ~x7 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_6 @noedge1_9 + @noedge6_9 + 2 d @obj + ;
        rup 1 ~x12 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_9 + @noedge3_9 + 2 d @obj + ;
        rup 1 ~x11 1 ~x10 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_7 + @noedge3_7 + 2 d @obj + ;
        rup 1 ~x11 >= 1 ;
        @obj soli x1 x2 x5 x8 ;
        rup 1 ~x8 1 ~x5 >= 1 ;
        pol @obj @noedge1_9 + ;
        rup 1 ~x8 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_7 + @noedge3_7 + 2 d @noedge2_4 @noedge2_9 + @noedge4_9 + 2 d +
            @noedge5_6 @noedge5_10 + @noedge6_10 + 2 d + @obj + ;
        rup >= 1 ;
        output NONE ;
        conclusion BOUNDS 8 8 ;
        end pseudo-Boolean proof ;
