Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



What The Revised Proof with Explicit Counting Looks Like
                                                                                                                Failing RUP steps have become pol
        pseudo-Boolean proof version 3.0                           derivations: add three non-adjacency
        @obj soli x7 x9 x12 ;                                      constraints together, divide by two, then
        rup 1 ~x12 1 ~x7 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_6 @noedge1_9 + @noedge6_9 + 2 d @obj + ;
                                                                   add the result to the solution-improving
        rup 1 ~x12 >= 1 ;                                          constraint
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_9 + @noedge3_9 + 2 d @obj + ;
        rup 1 ~x11 1 ~x10 >= 1 ;                                   The labels come straight out of the OPB
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_7 + @noedge3_7 + 2 d @obj + ;      file
        rup 1 ~x11 >= 1 ;
        @obj soli x1 x2 x5 x8 ;
        rup 1 ~x8 1 ~x5 >= 1 ;
        pol @obj @noedge1_9 + ;
        rup 1 ~x8 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_7 + @noedge3_7 + 2 d @noedge2_4 @noedge2_9 + @noedge4_9 + 2 d +
            @noedge5_6 @noedge5_10 + @noedge6_10 + 2 d + @obj + ;
        rup >= 1 ;
        output NONE ;
        conclusion BOUNDS 8 8 ;
        end pseudo-Boolean proof ;

What The Revised Proof with Explicit Counting Looks Like
        pseudo-Boolean proof version 3.0
        @obj soli x7 x9 x12 ;
        rup 1 ~x12 1 ~x7 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_6 @noedge1_9 + @noedge6_9 + 2 d @obj + ;
        rup 1 ~x12 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_9 + @noedge3_9 + 2 d @obj + ;
        rup 1 ~x11 1 ~x10 >= 1 ;                                         Three colour classes here, so three
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_7 + @noedge3_7 + 2 d @obj + ;            at-most-one constraints, all added to
        rup 1 ~x11 >= 1 ;                                                the solution-improving constraint
        @obj soli x1 x2 x5 x8 ;
        rup 1 ~x8 1 ~x5 >= 1 ;
        pol @obj @noedge1_9 + ;
        rup 1 ~x8 >= 1 ;
        pol @noedge1_3 @noedge1_7 + @noedge3_7 + 2 d @noedge2_4 @noedge2_9 + @noedge4_9 + 2 d +
            @noedge5_6 @noedge5_10 + @noedge6_10 + 2 d + @obj + ;
        rup >= 1 ;
        output NONE ;
        conclusion BOUNDS 8 8 ;
        end pseudo-Boolean proof ;

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Why a General-Purpose Proof System?

        Why not just put a “colouring bound” rule into the proof format?

                 There are dozens of different colouring algorithms
                 And extensions of these algorithms using MaxSAT reasoning

                 Even more for maximum weight clique-new-attempt, where weighted colour class rules get

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Why a General-Purpose Proof System?

        Why not just put a “colouring bound” rule into the proof format?

                 There are dozens of different colouring algorithms
                 And extensions of these algorithms using MaxSAT reasoning
                 Even more for maximum weight clique-new-attempt, where weighted colour class rules get
                 extremely clever (vertices can split their weight between several colours)
                 And we would need to do the same again for every other kind of solver

        We would rather not have a proof format and a proof checker per solver, per paper. . .
        Cutting planes to the rescue!
                 Turns out to be strong enough for all of these cases
                 Proof length scales linearly with solver reasoning time (asymptotically)


Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                           The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging           ACP Summer School ’26   98/128
Proof Logging Recap     VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming    Wrapping up

Proof Logging for Maximum Clique Solvers



Different Clique Algorithms

        Different search orders?
            ✓ Irrelevant for proof logging

        Using local search to initialise?
            ✓ Just log the incumbent

        Different bound functions?
                 Is cutting planes strong enough to justify every useful bound function ever invented?
                 So far, seems like it. . .

        Weighted cliques?
            ✓ Multiply a colour class by its largest weight
            ✓ Also works for vertices “split between colour classes”

Dominance in Clique Solving



Reducing the Trust Base

        We are still trusting rather a lot
                 That the proof checker is correct
                 That the pseudo-Boolean encoding really does describe the clique problem we were given

        Both can be dealt with:
                 VeriPB can elaborate a proof into a simpler kernel format
                 Uses only rules that the formally verified checker CakePB knows about
                 CakePB (written in CakeML and proved correct in HOL4)
                          reads the original graph file
                          does a formally verified translation to 0–1 ILP
                          checks the kernel proof against this translation

Dominance in Clique Solving



Reducing the Trust Base

        We are still trusting rather a lot
                 That the proof checker is correct
                 That the pseudo-Boolean encoding really does describe the clique problem we were given

        Both can be dealt with:
                 VeriPB can elaborate a proof into a simpler kernel format
                 Uses only rules that the formally verified checker CakePB knows about
                 CakePB (written in CakeML and proved correct in HOL4)
                          reads the original graph file
                          does a formally verified translation to 0–1 ILP
                          checks the kernel proof against this translation
                 So the answer is verified in terms of the original input

Dominance in Clique Solving



End-to-End Verification for Maximum Clique
        $ glasgow_clique_solver brock200_4.clq
        recursions = 56613
        clique = 17 (12 19 28 29 38 54 65 71 79 93 117 127 139 161 165 186 192)
        runtime = 0.02153s

        $ glasgow_clique_solver brock200_4.clq --prove brock200_4
        runtime = 0.266475s

        $ cake_pb_clique brock200_4.clq > brock200_4.verifiedopb

        $ veripb --elaborate brock200_4.corepb brock200_4.verifiedopb brock200_4.pbp
        s VERIFIED BOUNDS 183 <= obj <= 183

        $ cake_pb_clique brock200_4.clq brock200_4.corepb
        s VERIFIED MAX CLIQUE SIZE |CLIQUE| = 17

Subgraph Isomorphism in Pseudo-Boolean Form
         Each pattern vertex gets a target vertex:
                                   ∑︁
                                        𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 = 1                                                                 𝑝 ∈ V(𝑃)
                                                    𝑡 ∈V(𝑇 )

Proof Logging for Subgraph Isomorphism Solvers



Subgraph Isomorphism in Pseudo-Boolean Form
         Each pattern vertex gets a target vertex:
                                   ∑︁
                                        𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 = 1                                                                 𝑝 ∈ V(𝑃)
                                                    𝑡 ∈V(𝑇 )

         Each target vertex may be used at most once:
                                 ∑︁
                                      −𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 ≥ −1                                                                  𝑡 ∈ V(𝑇 )
                                                  𝑝 ∈V(𝑃 )

Proof Logging for Subgraph Isomorphism Solvers



Subgraph Isomorphism in Pseudo-Boolean Form
         Each pattern vertex gets a target vertex:
                                   ∑︁
                                        𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 = 1                                                                   𝑝 ∈ V(𝑃)
                                                     𝑡 ∈V(𝑇 )

         Each target vertex may be used at most once:
                                 ∑︁
                                      −𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 ≥ −1                                                                    𝑡 ∈ V(𝑇 )
                                                   𝑝 ∈V(𝑃 )

        Adjacency constraints: if 𝑝 maps to 𝑡, then neighbours of 𝑝 map to neighbours of 𝑡:
                               ∑︁
                      𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 +       𝑥𝑞,𝑢 ≥ 1               𝑝 ∈ V(𝑃), 𝑞 ∈ N(𝑝), 𝑡 ∈ V(𝑇 )
                                                 𝑢 ∈N(𝑡 )



Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                             The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                       ACP Summer School ’26     106/128
Proof Logging Recap     VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms               Constraint Programming       Wrapping up

Proof Logging for Subgraph Isomorphism Solvers


                                                                                                                  o   q            u        x
Degree Reasoning in Cutting Planes
                                                                                                                  p   r   t        v        y

                                                                                                                      s                     z




         Pattern vertex 𝑝 of degree deg(𝑝) can never be mapped to target vertex 𝑡 of degree < deg(𝑝)
         Observe N(𝑝) = {𝑞, 𝑟, 𝑠} and N(𝑡) = {𝑢, 𝑣 }
        We wish to derive 𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 ≥ 1

Proof Logging for Subgraph Isomorphism Solvers


                                                                                                                     o   q            u        x
Degree Reasoning in Cutting Planes
                                                                                                                     p   r   t        v        y

                                                                                                                         s                     z
                Adjacency:                                                         𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 + 𝑥𝑞,𝑢 + 𝑥𝑞,𝑣 ≥ 1
                                                                                   𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 + 𝑥𝑟,𝑢 + 𝑥𝑟,𝑣 ≥ 1
                                                                                   𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 + 𝑥𝑠,𝑢 + 𝑥𝑠,𝑣 ≥ 1
                Injectivity:                     −𝑥𝑜,𝑢 + −𝑥 𝑝,𝑢 + −𝑥𝑞,𝑢 + −𝑥𝑟,𝑢 + −𝑥𝑠,𝑢 ≥ −1
                                                 −𝑥𝑜,𝑣 + −𝑥 𝑝,𝑣 + −𝑥𝑞,𝑣 + −𝑥𝑟,𝑣 + −𝑥𝑠,𝑣 ≥ −1
                Literal axioms:                                                                          𝑥𝑜,𝑢 ≥ 0
                                                                                                         𝑥𝑜,𝑣 ≥ 0
                                                                                                         𝑥 𝑝,𝑢 ≥ 0
                                                                                                         𝑥 𝑝,𝑣 ≥ 0
                Add these together . . .
                                                                                                    3 · 𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 ≥ 1

                Adjacency:                                                         𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 + 𝑥𝑞,𝑢 + 𝑥𝑞,𝑣 ≥ 1
                                                                                   𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 + 𝑥𝑟,𝑢 + 𝑥𝑟,𝑣 ≥ 1
                                                                                   𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 + 𝑥𝑠,𝑢 + 𝑥𝑠,𝑣 ≥ 1
                Injectivity:                     −𝑥𝑜,𝑢 + −𝑥 𝑝,𝑢 + −𝑥𝑞,𝑢 + −𝑥𝑟,𝑢 + −𝑥𝑠,𝑢 ≥ −1
                                                 −𝑥𝑜,𝑣 + −𝑥 𝑝,𝑣 + −𝑥𝑞,𝑣 + −𝑥𝑟,𝑣 + −𝑥𝑠,𝑣 ≥ −1
                Literal axioms:                                                                          𝑥𝑜,𝑢 ≥ 0
                                                                                                         𝑥𝑜,𝑣 ≥ 0
                                                                                                         𝑥 𝑝,𝑢 ≥ 0
                                                                                                         𝑥 𝑝,𝑣 ≥ 0
                Add these together and divide by 3 to get
                                                                                                         𝑥 𝑝,𝑡 ≥ 1

Proof Logging for Subgraph Isomorphism Solvers



Degree Reasoning in VeriPB

         pol @adj_p_t_q @adj_p_t_r + @adj_p_t_s +                                                    % sum adjacency constraints
             @inj_u + @inj_v +                                                                       % sum injectivity constraints
             xo_u + xo_v +                                                                           % cancel stray xo_* >= 0
             xp_u + xp_v +                                                                           % cancel stray xp_* >= 0
             3 d ;                                                                                   % divide, and we're done

             @inj_u + @inj_v +                                                                       % sum injectivity constraints
             xo_u + xo_v +                                                                           % cancel stray xo_* >= 0
             xp_u + xp_v +                                                                           % cancel stray xp_* >= 0
             3 d ;                                                                                   % divide, and we're done

Proof Logging for Subgraph Isomorphism Solvers



Degree Reasoning in VeriPB

         pol @adj_p_t_q @adj_p_t_r + @adj_p_t_s +                                                    % sum adjacency constraints
             @inj_u + @inj_v +                                                                       % sum injectivity constraints
             xo_u + xo_v +                                                                           % cancel stray xo_* >= 0
             xp_u + xp_v +                                                                           % cancel stray xp_* >= 0
             3 d ;                                                                                   % divide, and we're done

        The labels come from the OPB file, so solver doesn’t need to keep track of constraint IDs
         Or we can let VeriPB do the last bit of tidying up for us:

         pol @adj_p_t_q @adj_p_t_r + @adj_p_t_s +                                                    % sum adjacency constraints
             @inj_u + @inj_v + ;                                                                     % sum injectivity constraints
         rup 1 ~xp_t >= 1 ;                                                                          % implied by the above

         Proof steps are “efficient” using cutting planes

Proof Logging for Subgraph Isomorphism Solvers



Proof Logging for Other Forms of Reasoning

         Possible to certify (almost) all of the other things state-of-the-art subgraph solvers do:
                  Conditional injectivity reasoning and filtering
                  Distance filtering
                  Neighbourhood degree sequences
                  Path filtering
                  Supplemental graphs

         Proof steps are “efficient” using cutting planes
                  Length of proof scales linearly with time complexity of the reasoning algorithms
                  Most proof steps require only trivial additional computations

         Code for the certifying Glasgow Subgraph Solver at
         https://github.com/ciaranm/glasgow-subgraph-solver

Integer Variables in Constraint Programming: (1/2)
        How to deal with integer variables in constraint programming?
        Given 𝐴 ∈ {−3, . . . , 9}, the direct encoding is:
                                                    𝑎 =−3 + 𝑎 =−2 + 𝑎 =−1 + 𝑎 =0 + 𝑎 =1 + 𝑎 =2 + 𝑎 =3
                                                                + 𝑎 =4 + 𝑎 =5 + 𝑎 =6 + 𝑎 =7 + 𝑎 =8 + 𝑎 =9 = 1

Integer Variables in Constraint Programming: (1/2)
        How to deal with integer variables in constraint programming?
        Given 𝐴 ∈ {−3, . . . , 9}, the direct encoding is:
                                                     𝑎 =−3 + 𝑎 =−2 + 𝑎 =−1 + 𝑎 =0 + 𝑎 =1 + 𝑎 =2 + 𝑎 =3
                                                                    + 𝑎 =4 + 𝑎 =5 + 𝑎 =6 + 𝑎 =7 + 𝑎 =8 + 𝑎 =9 = 1
        This doesn’t work for large domains. . .
        We could use a binary encoding:
                                                         −16𝑎 neg + 1𝑎 b0 + 2𝑎 b1 + 4𝑎 b2 + 8𝑎 b3 ≥ −3              and
                                               16𝑎 neg + −1𝑎 b0 + −2𝑎 b1 + −4𝑎 b2 + −8𝑎 b3 ≥ −9
        Bad properties for solver propagation, but that isn’t a problem for proof logging

Integer Variables in Constraint Programming: (1/2)
        How to deal with integer variables in constraint programming?
        Given 𝐴 ∈ {−3, . . . , 9}, the direct encoding is:
                                                     𝑎 =−3 + 𝑎 =−2 + 𝑎 =−1 + 𝑎 =0 + 𝑎 =1 + 𝑎 =2 + 𝑎 =3
                                                                    + 𝑎 =4 + 𝑎 =5 + 𝑎 =6 + 𝑎 =7 + 𝑎 =8 + 𝑎 =9 = 1
        This doesn’t work for large domains. . .
        We could use a binary encoding:
                                                         −16𝑎 neg + 1𝑎 b0 + 2𝑎 b1 + 4𝑎 b2 + 8𝑎 b3 ≥ −3              and
                                               16𝑎 neg + −1𝑎 b0 + −2𝑎 b1 + −4𝑎 b2 + −8𝑎 b3 ≥ −9
        Bad properties for solver propagation, but that isn’t a problem for proof logging
        Convention in what follows:
            Upper-case 𝐴, 𝐵, 𝐶 are CP variables used by solver
            Lower-case 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐 are corresponding Boolean variables used for PB encoding in proof log

Integer Variables in Constraint Programming: (2/2)
        We can mix binary and order encodings! Define big-M linear inequalities encoding

                                                   𝑎 ≥4 ⇔ −16𝑎 neg + 1𝑎 b0 + 2𝑎 b1 + 4𝑎 b2 + 8𝑎 b3 ≥ 4
                                                   𝑎 ≥5 ⇔ −16𝑎 neg + 1𝑎 b0 + 2𝑎 b1 + 4𝑎 b2 + 8𝑎 b3 ≥ 5
                                                    𝑎 =4 ⇔ 𝑎 ≥4 ∧ 𝑎 ≥5

Integer Variables in Constraint Programming: (2/2)
        We can mix binary and order encodings! Define big-M linear inequalities encoding

                                                   𝑎 ≥4 ⇔ −16𝑎 neg + 1𝑎 b0 + 2𝑎 b1 + 4𝑎 b2 + 8𝑎 b3 ≥ 4
                                                   𝑎 ≥5 ⇔ −16𝑎 neg + 1𝑎 b0 + 2𝑎 b1 + 4𝑎 b2 + 8𝑎 b3 ≥ 5
                                                    𝑎 =4 ⇔ 𝑎 ≥4 ∧ 𝑎 ≥5

        When creating 𝑎 ≥𝑖 , also introduce pseudo-Boolean constraints encoding

                                                                𝑎 ≥𝑖 ⇒ 𝑎 ≥ 𝑗             and 𝑎 ≥ℎ ⇒ 𝑎 ≥𝑖

        for the closest values 𝑗 < 𝑖 < ℎ that already exist

                                                   𝑎 ≥4 ⇔ −16𝑎 neg + 1𝑎 b0 + 2𝑎 b1 + 4𝑎 b2 + 8𝑎 b3 ≥ 4

        for the closest values 𝑗 < 𝑖 < ℎ that already exist
        We can do this:
                 Inside the pseudo-Boolean model where needed
                 Otherwise lazily during proof logging

Compiling Linear Inequalities


        Given inequality
                                                                             2𝐴 + 3𝐵 + 4𝐶 ≥ 42
        where 𝐴, 𝐵, 𝐶 ∈ {−3, . . . , 9}
        Encode in pseudo-Boolean form as

                                                           −32𝑎 neg + 2𝑎 b0 + 4𝑎 b1 + 8𝑎 b2 + 16𝑎 b3
                                                        + − 48𝑏 neg + 3𝑏 b0 + 6𝑏 b1 + 12𝑏 b2 + 24𝑏 b3
                                                         + − 64𝑐 neg + 4𝑐 b0 + 8𝑐 b1 + 16𝑐 b2 + 32𝑐 b3 ≥ 42

Compiling Table Constraints
        Constraints can be specified extensionally as list of feasible tuples, called a table
        Variable assignments must match some row in table
        Given table constraint
                                                          (𝐴, 𝐵, 𝐶) ∈ [(1, 2, 3), (1, 3, 4), (2, 2, 5)]
        define

                                   3𝑡 1 + 𝑎 =1 + 𝑏 =2 + 𝑐 =3 ≥ 3                                       i.e., 𝑡 1 ⇒ (𝑎 =1 ∧ 𝑏 =2 ∧ 𝑐 =3 )
                                   3𝑡 2 + 𝑎 =1 + 𝑏 =3 + 𝑐 =4 ≥ 3                                       i.e., 𝑡 2 ⇒ (𝑎 =1 ∧ 𝑏 =3 ∧ 𝑐 =4 )
                                   3𝑡 3 + 𝑎 =2 + 𝑏 =2 + 𝑐 =5 ≥ 3                                       i.e., 𝑡 3 ⇒ (𝑎 =2 ∧ 𝑏 =2 ∧ 𝑐 =5 )

        using newly introduced tuple selector variables

                                                                              𝑡1 + 𝑡2 + 𝑡3 = 1

Justifying Inference

        Key idea
        Anything the constraint programming solver knows must follow from unit propagation of guessed
        assignments on constraints in proof log

Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming       Wrapping up




Justifying Inference

        Key idea
        Anything the constraint programming solver knows must follow from unit propagation of guessed
        assignments on constraints in proof log

        If it follows from unit propagation on the encoding, nothing needed
        Some propagators and encodings need RUP steps for inferences
                 A lot of propagators are effectively “doing a little bit of lookahead” but in an efficient way




Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging          ACP Summer School ’26     120/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels   PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms   Constraint Programming       Wrapping up




Justifying Inference

        Key idea
        Anything the constraint programming solver knows must follow from unit propagation of guessed
        assignments on constraints in proof log

        If it follows from unit propagation on the encoding, nothing needed
        Some propagators and encodings need RUP steps for inferences
                 A lot of propagators are effectively “doing a little bit of lookahead” but in an efficient way

        A few need explicit cutting planes justifications written to the proof log
                 Linear inequalities just need to multiply and add
                 All-different needs a bit more


Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging          ACP Summer School ’26     120/128

Justifying All-Different Failures
           𝑉 ∈{1     4 5}
           𝑊 ∈{1 2 3    }                         𝑤 =1 + 𝑤 =2 + 𝑤 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑊 takes some value]
           𝑋 ∈{ 2 3     }                                𝑥 =2 + 𝑥 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑋 takes some value ]
           𝑌 ∈{1   3    }                         𝑦=1         + 𝑦=3                                           ≥   1   [ 𝑌 takes some value ]
           𝑍 ∈{1   3    }                         𝑧 =1        + 𝑧 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑍 takes some value ]

                      →                         −𝑣 =1 + −𝑤 =1 +         −𝑦=1 + −𝑧 =1 ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 1 ]
                          →                             −𝑤 =2 + −𝑥 =2                ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 2 ]
                               →                        −𝑤 =3 + −𝑥 =3 + −𝑦=3 + −𝑧 =3 ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 3 ]

Justifying All-Different Failures
           𝑉 ∈{1     4 5}
           𝑊 ∈{1 2 3    }                         𝑤 =1 + 𝑤 =2 + 𝑤 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑊 takes some value]
           𝑋 ∈{ 2 3     }                                𝑥 =2 + 𝑥 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑋 takes some value ]
           𝑌 ∈{1   3    }                         𝑦=1         + 𝑦=3                                           ≥   1   [ 𝑌 takes some value ]
           𝑍 ∈{1   3    }                         𝑧 =1        + 𝑧 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑍 takes some value ]

                      →                         −𝑣 =1 + −𝑤 =1 +         −𝑦=1 + −𝑧 =1 ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 1 ]
                          →                             −𝑤 =2 + −𝑥 =2                ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 2 ]
                               →                        −𝑤 =3 + −𝑥 =3 + −𝑦=3 + −𝑧 =3 ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 3 ]

                                                −𝑣 =1                                                         ≥ 1     [ Sum all constraints so far ]

Justifying All-Different Failures
           𝑉 ∈{1     4 5}
           𝑊 ∈{1 2 3    }                         𝑤 =1 + 𝑤 =2 + 𝑤 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑊 takes some value]
           𝑋 ∈{ 2 3     }                                𝑥 =2 + 𝑥 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑋 takes some value ]
           𝑌 ∈{1   3    }                         𝑦=1         + 𝑦=3                                           ≥   1   [ 𝑌 takes some value ]
           𝑍 ∈{1   3    }                         𝑧 =1        + 𝑧 =3                                          ≥   1   [ 𝑍 takes some value ]

                      →                         −𝑣 =1 + −𝑤 =1 +         −𝑦=1 + −𝑧 =1 ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 1 ]
                          →                             −𝑤 =2 + −𝑥 =2                ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 2 ]
                               →                        −𝑤 =3 + −𝑥 =3 + −𝑦=3 + −𝑧 =3 ≥ −1                             [ At most one variable = 3 ]

                                                −𝑣 =1                                                         ≥ 1     [ Sum all constraints so far ]
                                                 𝑣 =1                                                         ≥ 0     [ Variable 𝑣 =1 non-negative ]



Jakob Nordström (UCPH & LU)                                         The Joy of Pseudo-Boolean Proof Logging                              ACP Summer School ’26     121/128
Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels       PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms                       Constraint Programming       Wrapping up




Justifying All-Different Failures
           𝑉 ∈{1     4 5}
           𝑊 ∈{1 2 3    }                         𝑤 =1 + 𝑤 =2 + 𝑤 =3                                              ≥   1   [ 𝑊 takes some value]
           𝑋 ∈{ 2 3     }                                𝑥 =2 + 𝑥 =3                                              ≥   1   [ 𝑋 takes some value ]
           𝑌 ∈{1   3    }                         𝑦=1         + 𝑦=3                                               ≥   1   [ 𝑌 takes some value ]
           𝑍 ∈{1   3    }                         𝑧 =1        + 𝑧 =3                                              ≥   1   [ 𝑍 takes some value ]

                      →                         −𝑣 =1 + −𝑤 =1 +         −𝑦=1 + −𝑧 =1 ≥ −1                                 [ At most one variable = 1 ]
                          →                             −𝑤 =2 + −𝑥 =2                ≥ −1                                 [ At most one variable = 2 ]
                               →                        −𝑤 =3 + −𝑥 =3 + −𝑦=3 + −𝑧 =3 ≥ −1                                 [ At most one variable = 3 ]

                                                −𝑣 =1                                                             ≥ 1     [ Sum all constraints so far ]
                                                 𝑣 =1                                                             ≥ 0     [ Variable 𝑣 =1 non-negative ]

                                                        0                                                         ≥ 1     [ Sum above two constraints ]

Proof Logging Recap   VeriPB Proofs in Depth   Labels       PB Constraints     Reification    Subgraph Algorithms                       Constraint Programming       Wrapping up




Reformulation
        Auto-tabulation is possible
            Heavy use of extension variables

        Can re-encode max common subgraph as clique problem without changing PB encoding

                                                                                               𝑎 ↦→ { 1             2   3   4 }
                                                   a

                                                                                               4                                  4
                                         b     c        d
                                                                                               3                                  3
                                                                               𝑏 ↦→                                                   →↦ 𝑑
                                                                                               2                                  2
                                                                                               1                                  1
                                        1               2
                                        3               4
                                                                                               𝑐 ↦→ { 1             2   3   4 }

The Glasgow Constraint Solver
        Support for 37 (families of) constraints (and counting), with all inferences certified:

                 Extract information implied by scheduling problem constraints
                 Difference logic: Spot that a pile of two-term inequalities is a precedence network, and hand
                 to temporal propagator

The Glasgow Constraint Solver
        Support for 37 (families of) constraints (and counting), with all inferences certified:
        Abs, AllDifferent, AllEqual, Among, AtMostOne, BinPacking, Circuit, Comparison, Count, Cumulative, Diffn,
        Disjunctive, DivMod, Element, Equals, GCC, In, Increasing, Inverse, Knapsack, Lex, Linear, Logical, MDD,
        MinDistance, MinMax, Multiply, NValue, Parity, PlusMinus, Power, Regular, SeqPrecedeChain, SmartTable,
        Sort, Table, ValuePrecede

        Also certifies reformulations not written in but inferred from input:
                 Auto-tabulation: Identify small subproblem, solve it, and replace by table
                 Capacity strengthening by integrality, conflict cliques across resources, lifted cover cuts:
                 Extract information implied by scheduling problem constraints
                 Difference logic: Spot that a pile of two-term inequalities is a precedence network, and hand
                 to temporal propagator

Future Research Directions
        Performance of pseudo-Boolean proof logging and checking
            Faster proof logging and checking!
            Trim proofs while checking as in SAT solving [HHW13a] (upcoming work [ABB+ 26a])
            Compress proof files using binary format

Future Research Directions
        Performance of pseudo-Boolean proof logging and checking
            Faster proof logging and checking!
            Trim proofs while checking as in SAT solving [HHW13a] (upcoming work [ABB+ 26a])
            Compress proof files using binary format
        Proof logging for other combinatorial problems and techniques
            Model counting
            Mixed integer linear programming (suggested extension of VeriPB in [DEGH23])
            Satisfiability modulo theories (SMT) solving (Lots of work in, e.g., [HS22, BBC+ 23, RSB+ 26, RSS+ 26])

Future Research Directions
        Performance of pseudo-Boolean proof logging and checking
            Faster proof logging and checking!
            Trim proofs while checking as in SAT solving [HHW13a] (upcoming work [ABB+ 26a])
            Compress proof files using binary format
        Proof logging for other combinatorial problems and techniques
            Model counting
            Mixed integer linear programming (suggested extension of VeriPB in [DEGH23])
            Satisfiability modulo theories (SMT) solving (Lots of work in, e.g., [HS22, BBC+ 23, RSB+ 26, RSS+ 26])
        And more. . .
            Use proof logs for algorithm analysis or explainability purposes

Reminder about VeriPB Resources

        VeriPB tutorials, technical documentation, and more information at
        https://VeriPB.org

        Tutorial series with videos and slides from WHOOPS ’25 workshop at
        https://jakobnordstrom.se/WHOOPS25/

        Technical details on different proof logging techniques covered in research papers
        [EGMN20, GMN20, GMM+ 20, GN21, GMN22, GMNO22, VDB22, BBN+ 23, BGMN23, MM23,
        BBN+ 24, DMM+ 24, GMM+ 24, HOGN24, IOT+ 24, MMN24, DHN+ 25, JBBJ25, KLM+ 25, MM25,
        ABB+ 26a, ABB+ 26b, DKK+ 26, MNOT26, VCB26] and PhD theses [Goc22, McI26, Oer26]

        Lots of concrete example files at https://gitlab.com/MIAOresearch/software/VeriPB

Summing up
        Second Half of Lecture
            More technical details on VeriPB (mostly for reference)
            Some convenient syntactic sugar
            Example applications
                      1     Maximum clique
                      2     Subgraph isomorphism
                      3     Constraint programming (much more about this tomorrow, including hands-on exercises)
        Main message
           Combinatorial solvers are amazing, but ensuring correctness is crucial
           Certifying solvers producing machine-verifiable proofs is the most promising approach
           Pseudo-Boolean proof logging has been surprisingly successful in moving the research
           frontier forward
